ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u8	Thank you, Mr President. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the Greek Parliament is today introducing an emblematic bill of paramount importance to the Greek people, whose vote will mark the passage to another era. It is a bill that facilitates the vote of Greeks abroad, a top issue for which we, the Members of this House, are given the historic opportunity to do what is self-evident and to restore an injustice that lasted decades in response to our moral and national duty. That is why it is a great honour for me to recommend it today on behalf of the Government of New Democracy. After all, our historic past dictates us to move in this direction, when a hundred and fifty-seven years ago, in 1862, Mr. Zaimis, Minister for the Interior, with special legislation, made sure that Greeks from abroad vote for the first time. It took us more than a hundred and fifty years to overcome any controversy and with understanding and sense of responsibility to find the golden intersection, so as to make it easier for Greeks abroad to participate in our country's political process. [[At this point, the First Vice-President of Parliament, Mr Nikitas Kaklamanis, occupyes the President-in-Office of the Council.]] After all, the conditions for this step have matured, and Greece cannot be the exception, as in all twenty-eight Member States of the European Union only two of them do not regulate voting rights for citizens outside their territory, with our country, unfortunately, belonging to this sad minority. Greece, ladies and gentlemen, needs as much as ever in a very critical period of global Hellenism, our compatriots who left decades ago. They are those who, when necessary, defend our national interests where international public opinion is formed, which can be a force of foreign policy support, as they have a significant influence on being in many cases calculated electoral power in the countries in which they live. They are those who can financially support our country by contributing to the well-appointed development, those who managed to distinguish themselves in every sector and in their faces, are the ones who, although living decades abroad, keep the flag of Greece high and weep in the resurgence of our national anthem. But homeland also needs the thousands of young people who, in the years of the crisis of the last decade, have left massively for the purpose of seeking work. We didn't manage to keep these children in our homeland, and that's what they've been looking for. It is a youth with knowledge, intelligence, critically thinking, inclined for work and for life, and this youth observes us. We must show them that there is still hope in the country. We need these young people as before. And since vague announcements and good intentions are not enough, the first step to bring these children home closer is to give them the minimum, the right to codecision with us on the modulation of the election result. Moreover, the electoral right of Greeks abroad was constitutionally guaranteed in the Constitution of 1975 and also in that of 2001, without having been implemented, as the law is needed to be voted in favour by two parliamentarians, so that the constitutional provision is to date an empty letter. The new Republic has repeatedly tabled proposals for legislation in the past, but also proposals for legislation, without the necessary parliamentary majority being achieved. The President of the New Democracy and Prime Minister of the country, Kyriakos Mitsotakis, loyal to his election commitment, raised the issue with the government's priorities and immediately started discussions with the political leaders. Discussions have resulted in the establishment of a intergroup committee, representing all the parties in Parliament, where, after adequate dialogue, a basic framework of agreement with four main axes was established. The position of the New Democracy, from the outset of this dialogue, was that all Greeks abroad must vote without exceptions and conditions, and indeed by letter vote. The Government did not make any reductions from its initial positions, but the other option was the deadlock and given the necessary majority of the two-member parliamentarians, as provided for in the Constitution, compromises were made, with the look at the great institutional aspect to be implemented. The first step towards the implementation of this agreement has recently been taken by this House, with the revision of Article 54 of the Constitution and the related addition of paragraph 4, with which constitutional provisions of today's draft law have been shielded. The second and decisive step will be to vote in favour of this draft law. The current regulation on the facilitation of the Greeks completes the framework for facilitating these voters and gives the principle of universality of voting, enshrined in Article 51 of the Constitution, an essential and dynamic dimension. The Greek state thus fulfils its constitutional duty, ensuring that universality is in fact guaranteed and constitutional provisions are given full and effective regulatory radiation. Ladies and gentlemen, it is true that this bill, as it is now introduced into its final form, is the result of a fruitful dialogue, as this dialogue has also been developed at the meetings of the relevant committees. There have been issues on which different opinions have been expressed and discussions have been held, which, after compromises and concessions, have resulted in the specific provisions, as they have finally been formulated and introduced today. A crucial factor in the formulation of the final text was the hearing of the bodies, with the participation of the representatives of the family. The representatives of our foreign compatriots came to two meetings of the committees, expressed their views and heard the voice of the family. This draft is structured in two parts. The first is to facilitate the exercise of the electoral right of voters outside the Greek territory, in the national elections, in the European elections and in the course of a national referendum, and the second is to amend the electoral law for the election of MPs. The main axes of the first part, which incorporate the agreement of the Intergroup and the processing that took place in the committees are the following four: First, it is defined which of the Greeks abroad can exercise their electoral right at their home and under which conditions. Firstly, they must already be registered in the general electoral lists of the municipality of the Hellenic Republic and, in addition, they must be met cumulatively and the following two conditions: first, they must have a total of two years in Greece, during the last thirty-five years, irrespective of the time they leave. Acknowledgement of the two-year stay, which is not required to be continuous, will be demonstrated by public documents, in particular by a certificate of Greek school of each grade, a certificate of payment of insurance contributions or a certificate of military service. Secondly, they must have made a tax declaration, either in the current or the previous tax year. This condition is met by submitting only a simple tax declaration, namely by submitting only form E1, without having to have any assets and assets in Greece. It is particularly important that the submission of a tax declaration is not required for voters who have not completed the 30th year of their age, but provided that they have made a first-degree tax declaration in the current or the previous tax year. With this exception, Greeks who left abroad in the years of the crisis are given a substantial facility so that they can exercise their electoral right under the minimum conditions. The second axis is the way in which the electoral right is exercised. The permanent position of the New Democracy, but also, admittedly, other parties, was the establishment of a letter of vote, a request put forward by the representatives of the family. In the context of the necessary majority of the two hundred votes, however, the provision provides for the presence of the electorate in elections, to be set up in embassies, consulates or other voting places. Here too, it is very important that the Minister's commitment is to make every effort, in consultation with the states abroad, where Greeks live, so that, if necessary, other appropriate places will be provided to carry out this electoral process. The ultimate aim is for our compatriots to vote as close as possible to their homes and at the lowest possible financial burden and suffering. The third axis provides that the vote of the voters concerned will count equally on the overall election result and therefore also on the total distribution of seats. The fourth presupposes that foreign voters will vote on the party's dominance ballot, but without having the right to a crucifixion in the general parliamentary elections. However, in the European elections they will express a preference among the voters, in a manner comparable to the voters who exercise their right to vote within the country. At this point, it is particularly critical that the legislative improvement submitted by the expedited Ministry, according to which three of the fifteen members of each party's roll-call vote will come from the split, and one of these three candidates will be placed in the first three positions of the ballot. Thus, with the new regulation, an independent right to be elected for the Greeks of the diaspora is introduced, thus being met and analogously demand from the family and their representation is strengthened in the Greek Parliament. The other provisions of Part One describe how the electorate should register the special electoral lists abroad, the timing and the way in which the vote is held and other details. In particular, the request to register the voter will be made electronically, at a special portal of the Ministry of the Interior, which will be attached to the documents required, and the information available in public databases will be obtained automatically, with the consent of the elector. It also provides for the setting up of a Special Intergroup Committee, which is responsible, on the one hand, to consider objections to rejection decisions on requests for registration rejected and, on the other, to carry out sampling checks on those requests for registration. This regulation eliminates even the slightest concern about the validity and transparency of the whole procedure. The vote will take place between Wednesday and Saturday, before Sunday of the national elections, and will be completed in one day. The counting and announcement of the results will be carried out at the same time as that of the Greek territory, since the files are airbornely sent to Athens and specifically to the Central Bank of Foreign Electoral Elections at the Athens Court of Appeal, but have not been blocked. The second part of the bill attempts targeted interventions in the electoral law for Members' election, with the main aim of simplifying the procedure and aligning it with modern technological developments. Some of the basic innovations introduced are: the increase in the number of Members of the State from 12 to 15, by adjusting the number of other Members, the introduction of an electronic procedure for the submission of applications, the provision for compensation to members of the commissions, the obligation for legal representatives to transmit during the vote the number of votes cast, so that public opinion on the course of the electoral process is informed, the simplification of the procedure for resignation from posts which are a blocking of election, the replacement of the paper version of the electoral lists, which will now only be sent to a digital means of unique registration. Ladies and gentlemen, this bill is the result of agreement and cooperation between the parties. Finally, we have succeeded in finding common stumbling and the opportunity is given to establish a political consensus culture, a station in our parliamentary life. It is one of the few bills built into another logic, the logic of national understanding in the name of national unity. There is no excuse for his vote anymore. The attitude of each of us towards the bill will put us before our historical responsibility. And let us not forget that our country's history has taught us that unity is the way to overcome difficulties. So let us present today a strong message of unity to the Greeks of all the world that are watching us, by voting in favour of the bill with the greatest possible majority, in a critical and charged period for our national issues. Ladies and gentlemen, Victor Hugo said that "the world is Greece that is expanding". So I urge you today to overcome the party limits, to cross the border and to become the bridge that will unite the universal Hellenism with the country. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u10	Thank you very much, Mr President. The question of regulating the vote of Greeks abroad has been a constitutional requirement since 1975. Why has it not been arranged so far, and why do I ask this question at first? A prerequisite for approaching every national issue - and this is a national issue - is to deal with it in terms of honesty and not hypocrisy, because the necessary fronts are being built on this very basis. The truth is that none of the two parties, which were ruling alternately between themselves by 2015, did want to resolve the issue. In addition, an increased majority is foreseen only after the 2001 Constitutional Review. Why? Why are the parties that are today being cut off for the need to vote - and we agree with them - not to explain why they did not do so in the previous period? The answer is obvious. I refer to the criticism made by Evangelos Venizelos in the respective proposal for a Law of 2001 in the New Republic which had similar characteristics to the proposal originally tabled. Read what he says there about working the payroll, trying to become a game in internal party games. We are not like this, ladies and gentlemen. We are the only party that, when he came to the government, sought to solve the issue seriously and across the cross-party consensus. We have not only raised the question of the revision of Article 54 - we have put it, not you - but we have also anticipated the setting-up of a legislative committee, so that, with the participation of experts and on the basis of the proposals of the avenues, we can have the bill. The setting-up of that committee as provided for in Article 256 of Law 455/2018 was of general support. Apart from us and the ANELs - the then government majority - and the Communist Party of Greece as well as the Communist Party of Greece, with a tolerance of the Potami and a vote 'against', yours, gentlemen of the New Democracy. You, who today appear to be the protagonists of the vote on the motion for a resolution, are the ones who have not regulated this issue for for forty years and who, when it came to the opportunity to regulate it with a cross-party consensus, have taken the lead. And hypocrisy doesn't stop here. Your initial position was epic vote without criterion, generalised vote of Greeks abroad. And what did you tell us? That this proposal only relates to three hundred and fifty thousand registered on the electoral lists. National measures cannot be built on assumptions. I watch my expressions because I don't want to be acid. You knew that this figure is not accurate, not only because the New Democracy as a government in 2007 argued with the European Court of Justice, arguing to restrict it to what ends in the bill of voting facility, that voters abroad are three and a half million, but also because you know that on the basis of the Code of citizenship and the law of blood that it establishes, the potential voters, those who can take Greek citizenship and vote - because there is no intermediate stage, all Greeks, all who have Greek citizenship vote - amount to multiple millions. At the hearing we heard for eight million, only from one continent. Why, then, have you had to resort to this concealment of the truth? For the simple reason that there is no European experience and global country with a significant dispersion such as ours, which introduces such a generalised voting facility for the deceased. Those countries which have a significant dispersion such as us or a greater dispersion from us, such as Ireland, Cyprus, Israel, Armenia, are reaching an end which we have never adopted and completely excludes outside residents from voting, even when they are in the country. We obviously want the elected representatives to have a voice, a vote and a right to vote, but on the basis of and criteria for the democratic principle and European experience. The reason why the countries mentioned above do not introduce a generalised voting facility, as you are proposing, is as follows. It is obvious that when there is a multiple electorate outside the territory and the generalised voting facility was given to him, that would actually decide on elections. It would, in short, alter the will of the electorate made up of the country's permanent residents. For this reason, what did the European Court of Human Rights say? That this right is acceptable. I read you from the Sitaropoulos decision against Greece: "Taking into account the presumption that citizens who do not live within the territory are not affected so directly or constantly by the daily problems of their country and have less information about them, secondly, the close relationship between the right to vote in the parliamentary elections and that someone directly affected by the decisions of the political bodies elected in this way, and thirdly, the legitimate care of the legislature to restrict the influence of citizens living abroad in elections, which, while they are admittedly fundamental, are mainly those who live within the country". So in view of this, what is generalised, absolute European practice? Two systems prevail, weighted in one way or another. The first is the so-called closed representation system. They elect a number of their own representatives within regions that meet their permanent establishment. This is the system implemented by France, Portugal and Italy. That is, as I will tell you next, and what the National Committee on Human Rights and the Venice Committee, the competent institution of the Council of Europe and the Council of De-Democrat Hellenism, presided over when he had expressed his views. This was precisely the proposal that SYRIZA tabled in the Constitutional Review of Article 54, that is to say there should be up to five local elections, obviously after a census where representatives of our compatriots who live there would be elected, so that they do not only have the right to vote, which remains disabled if it is not accompanied by the right to vote. I read to you, in evidence of my claim that this is the consistent practice of the Council of Europe, but also what the institutions associated with our own country have asked for, what the Committee on Human Rights has proposed in a report which one of its authors was the colleague and fellow Minister, Mr Gerapitris, the Minister of State: 'Institution of representation in cases of a large number of non-national voters', obviously like our country,'recommends best practice, which defenses the consequences of the mass involvement of non-national voters in national elections, by recognising the latter's right to representation through a predetermined number of Members or senators'. And, as I said before, the Council of Greek Democrats had also proposed that they should elect their own representatives. You will find this in the CSCE Bureau report on the text of the working document 'Using the electoral right in the general parliamentary elections'. So that was the proposal that we tabled and we still consider it best today. A proposal was tabled by the Communist Party of Greece which also has the backing for European practice. But it is not usually followed by countries that have a great deal of dispersal like us. It is the choice of England, Germany, some Scandinavian countries, to introduce restrictions on the actual bond that the elector has in terms of facilitating the exercise of the right. We agreed to discuss on the basis of the proposal of the Communist Party of Greece. Why? Firstly, because we want a solution, and secondly, because it does not differ from our original conception, which wants the vote to be weighted in some way. Why? In order to respect the democratic principle, in order not to alter the will of the electorate of permanent residents. That was the'red line' we had set in the first place. The New Democracy has been strategically shifted in this respect, precisely because its initial position was different, contrary to the common European legal culture, and in fact contrary, as I have said, to the proposals of the Council. It established in the original version a generalized right to vote without a right to representation. Interestingly, the explanatory statement adopts our logic and demonstrates our claim for a strategic displacement of the position of the government party. I read you from the suggestion: "The restrictions put in the Constitutional Review on a proposal from the Communist Party of Greece, "as security disputes, aim to prevent any intervention on the election result to a sufficient degree", note the phrase "to alter the will of the electorate", our logic, "and this is because the total number of off-national Greeks lies on the basis of considerations as to whether it is legitimately legitimate to intervene decisively in the formation of the election result. These constitutional tolerable restrictions bring about a weighting of the result, once again a word that was criticised, as an anti-constitutional phrase, which I had used, by Mr Gerapititi, "which allows the exercise of the right by non-national voters, on the other hand, cannot alter the electoral behaviour of the voters in dominance". We have therefore come to the debate on this basis, because I repeat that we want to have the voices, votes and the right to be elected. We have tabled proposals for specific and written measures in a particularly rough draft law, which was unfair to the seriousness of the matter. Some of the initial shortcomings in the draft were covered in the Committee's discussions. There are still major problems, which I shall be addressing, but also legal cases, which the Scientific Service of the Parliament also points out. The original anti-constitutional provision for jurisdiction in the control of disputes in relation to the lists of public jurisdiction was corrected by the proper court, the administrative court, but the word “public jurisdiction” remained in law, as if there were voluntary jurisdiction in the administrative courts. I am referring to this as one of the examples of sloppyness in drafting the plan, but because my time is already running out, I am passing on to the essentials. What did we ask for in committee? Consist two things. One was the imprecise and transparency of the process, self-evident for anyone who respects democracy. The second is to grant an independent right to be elected to the migrants. Steps have been taken in both directions. There has been an ad hoc committee which, because there is no doubt in this respect in the report by the Scientific Office of the Parliament, please, Minister, confirm that it has a decisive responsibility to monitor the procedure. It was also partially accepted, not in the way we would like, but in any case as one step, that the members of parliament can elect their own representative by means of a charter of sovereignty, not on the patriotism of the parties, but on the basis of a concrete commitment. However, two serious issues remain, which we hope will be dealt with by the government at the end, precisely because they do not relate to any party feasibility but to the need to serve democracy and transparency and transparency and, in general, to the good running of the elections. One is the renewal of electoral lists. It is planned for a ten-year duration. We have no precedent in Greek law. And not only do we have no precedent, but 10 years of voting power means that the thirty-five years that we have agreed to be acceptable limiting...

ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u16	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the last few days are the culmination of the debate over the last few months, after the collective effort to try once again as a political system, as political parties, as political persons with the role of the legislator, to withdraw to the extent of the circumstances and to resolve the issue of the vote by the Greek Democrats, within the framework of national duty alone. In recent months, it has been the continuation of our political system of fearful and inappropriate, chained to the mast of forgetting, so as not to give in to the sirens that he saw and felt that they were showing him the way, in fact one-way, to restore a great political injustice both according to law and morality. For many years he resisted and never succeeded in reaching the required number of two hundred that would give the legislative way out of the matter. As soon as the issue of the vote of the results came to the forefront, the Party Precision weighed again on fire. Additions, removals, multiplications and divisions became the real spaskhead, mainly of the system's self-progressive ones, which eventually led them to political neurosis, self-insulation and resuscitative cysts. How many of them are out of the city? How many will vote? What party will they choose? Will the election result really be altered with an impact on us? Did the lesser the better and safer? These people, although they come from Greece, although they love Greece - no one disputes this - but because of their long stay abroad - most may have been born abroad, they may have been descendants of old immigrants - they have objectively integrated into the societies of these countries, they have linked their lives and their future to these societies and most importantly are that they form a criterion and a political conscience based on reality and developments in these countries. ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u38	Thank you very much, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, the bill under discussion is essentially divided into two parts. The first concerns the vote on the vote and the second amends the existing provisions relating to the electoral procedure. The request to facilitate the exercise of the electoral right of Greeks who live abroad is consistent and needs to be met. As Mera25, recognizing the need and feeling the pain of the deceased, as well as their desire to have an active role in the political activities of our country, we tabled our own integrated proposal, which solved the issue of the election, as well as of the elected citizen, for our fellow citizens who live outside the Greek territory. The main elements of this proposal were the separation of the recent immigrants, people, who had to leave in the years of the crisis - schematically from 2010 onwards - from those who have been permanently living abroad for twenty, thirty or more years. For these two categories, we have proposed the following: firstly, recent immigrants can vote from their home place for the territory they belonged to before they left Greece. Our fellow citizens who live, on the other hand, for years outside, to be able to vote for candidates for the local elections that should be created, Australia, New Zealand, North America and others. In this way they would be able to send to the Greek Parliament people who know and achieve a dual goal, on the one hand, the Greek Parliament would have the voice of the offenders through elected representatives of the family and, on the other, the links between the deportees and Greece would be further strengthened. Unfortunately, despite the initial positive attitude to co-operation with which we came to the meetings of the intergroup set up under the relevant Minister for the Interior, we were unable to reach a final agreement, since there are several points in the draft law - thorns, which, even after the legislative improvements, do not allow us to do anything else, rather than vote against the bill at first. Let me explain why. First of all, the bill provides for the possibility of voting from the place of residence, with the vote counting the final result, but without any possibility of crossing the national elections. Our non-national fellow citizens will only be able to vote for a list of sovereigns, namely only a party, without selecting specific representatives. This creates an unequal category of voters with reduced voting rights. It goes on to say that the Ministry facilitates the exercise of the election, while in essence and practically we deprive our fellow citizens of a right they retain if they finally choose to come to Greece to vote. We are therefore saying that we are facilitating them by exchanging the possibility of voting from their place of residence in exchange for crusade. By selecting the prestige list, Greeks abroad will be the only Greeks who will not have the right to choose MPs who, as I said before, retain if they return to our country to vote. With the government proposal they will only vote for a party, which introduces an incomprehensible state of discrimination and inequality between and outside Greece. Indeed, it is worth noting that, in the European elections, the voters retain the right to crusade, since they are in a country of the European Union, and in the event of a referendum, they have the same rights as the voters in existence. This draft law, in accordance with the provisions of Article 2, lays down the two basic conditions to be met in order to enable it to be placed on the special electoral lists. These are the following: first, a proven two-year presence in the country over the last thirty-five years, a presence that can function cumulatively, and, second, a submission of E1, E2, E3 or E9, in the current or previous tax year. As a PARTA25, we consider it unacceptable that the possession of property should be linked to the right to vote. Also, if a person has made a zero tax statement, we still have not received an answer whether or not he can finally vote. The same article provides that the depopulation will remain on the lists for ten years, without any provision for what will happen after them and how the list will be updated. At the same time, if one of the out-of-democratics actually completes the process and registers on the special electoral lists and happens to be found in Greece on the day of the elections, he will only be able to vote if he or she goes on to remove it from the special lists. Article 4 sets out how to certify the two-year stay, which is the certificate of attendance at a school in Greece, the fulfilment of a military service or the payment of insurance contributions to the country. We believe that it is declarable that the right to vote should be linked to the obligation to work, and it is also unacceptable that the Minister in question should be allowed to modify these criteria, according to what will work for the benefit of his party. Moving on to Article 7, we have the prediction that the minimum number of foreign elections is forty, and we can only ask ourselves whether there will be a provision if there are fewer than 40 Greeks living in the area, but yet they still want to vote. Article 10 provides that emigrants will only be offered sovereign lists, turning them into second-class voters. We cannot attempt to promote, as an incentive to exercise the democratic right to vote, a cut in that, by adopting this only alternative to voting on the rule of law. Article 12 is a reasonable regulation of the electoral law, but it accompanies the fact that non-Greek Greeks will only be able to vote for a sovereign vote, although in the end, the election of representatives will depend on the overall percentage that a party will receive, taking into account its percentage within and outside Greece. The second part of the bill contains mainly provisions aimed at facilitating the electoral process and articles which are mostly procedural, seeking to harmonise the electoral procedure with technological developments, as well as simplify the procedures involved. The main innovations it introduces are the creation of an electronic portal for the registration of candidates, which will replace the current performance process with a judicial curator, as well as the proclamation at a public meeting from Section I of the Ice Bay, which will cover the proclamation of combinations of parties, coalitions, collaborative parties, independent and individual candidates. Article 26 abolishes the distinction between a register of males and a budget, creating a new civic register, while Article 27 minimizes the deadline for declaring emblems and names and highlights the obligation of the Ministry of Interior to post candidates on its website. Next, Article 28 proceeds to shorten the deadlines for the resolution of any dispute, at the same time removing any risk of the smooth holding of the elections. Article 29 provides for a nomination declaration via the electronic portal of candidates, while Article 30 describes the procedure for accessing it. Article 31 lays down the amount of the electoral offender by rounding it up to EUR 150. As MERA25, we are against any financial payment for the exercise of the right to vote, so we shall vote against it. In Article 32, following a legislative improvement, it is foreseen that one fifth of the candidates for a party's vote should be covered by emigrants, at least one of whom should be placed in one of the first three posts. With this provision, the parties are obliged to place five Greeks abroad on their top roll-calls, with one potentially in a election position. The provision in itself is not a negative one, but it is a long way from the analogy, which was a pillar of our own proposal, according to which the seats that Members of the constituencies of Greek electorate would have been a function of the registered voters in each of them. Article 33 goes hand in hand with Article 36, which defines the extension of the rule of law, of the twelve people who now have 15. As MERA25, we are opposed to the concept of the rule of law in itself, as we have mentioned in detail in our election programme. Article 28 provides for a daily allowance to be paid to the members of the committee of inquiry, a provision which we undoubtedly consider to be positive, as it will solve the problem of the non-commissioning of the committees to carry out their duties on the day of the elections. While Rule 39, which we also consider to be positive, extends the closure of the appointment of taxing committees and to former Members of Parliament, members of government, etc. Article 41 lays down the declaration of alternate Members, who will no longer be made by the competent Court of First Instance but by the Marios Ice. Subsequently, Article 42 extends the framework of persons who can perform and communicate, adding officials of municipal police and municipal officials to them. The provisions of Article 43 give heterodists, who wish to exercise their electoral right at their place of residence, the opportunity to apply for it through an electronic request, thus greatly reducing the bureaucracy that has been in force so far. Ladies and gentlemen, the out-of-presidents are close to Greece. They are following developments, they are learning about the current situation, they are part of the country and ambassadors from abroad, so we must make it easier for them to exercise their rights established by the Constitution. We must make it easier for them to vote from their place of residence, but it is our duty to do so, taking the real defence of their rights and not trying to convince them that we are helping them. Unfortunately, this draft law deprives them of a fundamental right, that of crusade, making second-class voters on the altar of voting at the place of residence. At the same time, it is being promoted as an extra facilitator to join the prestige ballot, by trying to side with the fact that, now, a candidate can be a candidate to any party he wishes, whether in the dominance ballot or in a region. So we don't give them anything new, there's just going to be a communication use of a right that already exists. At the same time, we must recognise all the positive elements which emerged from the process of the intergroup meeting, starting with the addition to Article 54 of the Constitution, so as to allow the future creation of the local elections, which is a central point of our own proposal. We continue with the use of the TAXISNET platform to declare registration in the special lists, which we have recognised as suitable for such use. I could also not leave the government's original thoughts behind for a dictatorial vote, since we from the outset felt that it was an insolvent solution, due to the lack - in the current period of time - of the necessary safeguards to safeguard confidentiality and the inequitable nature of the vote. Mer25 produced an integrated proposal, which is far from the appropriate governmental one, which ensured both the right to vote and the right to stand for Greek citizens in a proportionate and fair manner. That is why, as I have already said, we cannot, in principle, vote against the bill, as well as all the provisions that are increasing, rather than solving the problem, while at the same time supporting articles which regulate procedural matters in a positive way. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u40	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the possibility of Greeks living abroad to exercise their electoral right, although self-evident, was neither easy nor given. I would like to tell them, however, that precisely this pursuit of a solution which would satisfy everyone was one of the reasons why, for decades, progress on this issue has been zero. With our differences, we have ignored the fact that many times solutions start with a minimum common denominator, that you cannot pursue the maximum, when you cannot guarantee the minimum, that something can start to a lesser extent, in order to see how it works in practice, to see if and where we can improve it. This is the way the current government has chosen. He chose the option instead of the ideal, different from nothing or almost the past. And she chose to respond quickly within the first five months of her term of office. He chose not to fortify the most advanced positions, which would condemn all efforts to fail, such as in 2009, when he had brought such a bill to the House, which, unfortunately, voted for only a hundred and fifty colleagues, and which the coalition had voted against or as in 2016, 2017 and 2019, when as a Professional Opposition, we tabled again and again the same proposal of law, which was clearly ignored by the SYRIZA government. Ladies and gentlemen, today is a new beginning. In itself, voting on the bill is an important step. We have a starting point, a reference point. The challenge from now on is double: First of all, we must make the law as operational as possible in its individual forecasts, as must, for example, with the electoral departments, which must be set up in order to serve the highest possible number of voters. The second challenge is, once we have seen the results in practice, to be able to agree on subsequent changes that will echo the feeling and expectations of even more of our compatriots. That is why we call on them to respond to this first very important step, because it is their own interest, their own turnout, which will send out the message to those who have not dared enough to dare the future. Thank you.

With the proposal put forward by the JRC on facilitating Greek voters abroad, we believe that, not only do all these people who have migrated to the years of crisis and the memorials, but that they are also covered and facilitated to vote in their place of residence, even those who have left several years before the crisis and have not been able to return to the country because of this crisis, although they still have such a desire and expectation. From the outset, however, we have said about this facilitation that it must be subject to certain criteria to prevent the electorate from being altered by voters whose perceptions and decisions are often formed in a completely different political and social environment or by voters without, or with little knowledge of, modern Greek reality or with no real ties to the country or, of course, with voters who are not affected by any policy being implemented in Greece, nor will they suffer the slightest consequence of any election, because their lives are, after all, solely linked to the political and social reality of Greece, but of the country in which they live. Was it so unlikely that this alteration of the electorate would occur with an over-inflation of it? Not at all. Based on the existing law of citizenship and naturalisation in our country, which is based on the anachronistic concept of blood law and which in our country leads us to self-righteous lists - which is not the case, for example, in other European countries - this could happen even with distant third-generation descendants, who would simply have a Greek ancestor, without them having to do with Greek reality. Some come here and show the existence of mental and emotional links with Greece. Not only are these ties questioned, but they are all worthy and respected, but they are not enough to establish the facilitation of electoral rights, a basic political right, which at its core, and according to the urban perception, links the consequences of exercising this right to anyone exercising it. This is not only supported by the Communist Party of Greece, it is not arbitrary. It is an international position and practice and, indeed, countries such as Germany, Great Britain and the United States. In other words, countries that are usually offended by you as standards. Here are all three criteria proposed by our party to facilitate Greek voters abroad, which met widely socially. This was reflected in recent polls both inside and outward. These criteria are: First, the proven existence and functioning of real and substantial links with the country, for example economic, tax, social. Secondly, the total, and not necessarily continuous, two-year stay in Greece for the last thirty-five years. Germany, for example, is more stringent here. The limit is at 15 years. Thirty-five years - let this misunderstanding no longer be a limit, but it is the timeframe within which the two-year stay must be proved. Thirdly, the self-representation of the voter in front of the ballot, not the vote by correspondence or the vote by electronic votes, which neither the secrecy of the vote nor the validity of the election procedure guarantee, which was previously seen - for those who remember - in the Italian elections with the letter of vote that the mafia shared. Most of all, however, these practices of letter and electronic voting degrade and undermine the essence of political participation. Some, these criteria have even emerged as bureaucratic obstacles. In any case, there is much less of these obstacles to providing for example the heating allowance or other social benefits. In conclusion, I would like to say that these criteria neither limit nor remove anyone's electoral right. They are simply the prerequisites for the exercise of the electoral right, through the inclusion in the special electoral lists of the Greeks abroad, and ultimately operate in the interest of the voters themselves, against divisive logic that may be created in marginal elections. Finally, we hope that this bill will become a guide to the Greek national voters, Minister, because in last July's elections thousands of employees in the tourism sector have not only been facilitated to exercise this right, but in fact their employer has banned it. Thank you.

The most appropriate way to achieve this was to establish the letter vote, as has been done for years in all democratic and civilised states in the world. Our Constitution, as has been the case since 2001, requires 200 votes to implement this facility. This means that if these votes are not gathered, the law cannot be passed by the House and so we would lose another great opportunity. Finally, the completion of the revision of Article 54 of the Constitution, so that the restrictions in point 4 are compatible with the Constitution. Ladies and gentlemen, the vote of this bill requires it by history. Our country, through the values and ideas it gave birth to and the global cultural heritage it offered in the world, is very great to close it at the narrow geographical limits. All three quarters of the forty-seven Member States of the Council of Europe have enacted the vote on their counterparts from the place of residence in their national elections. Countries to which I referred at the outset provide either for voting in polling centres in their region, usually embassies or consulate, or both. ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u103	Thank you, Mr. Stefanides. Mr Ljupi has the floor from New Democracy and then Mr Zebbles also from New Democracy.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u104	Thank you, Mr President. Mr Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, the bill that comes to be passed today after the revision of Article 54 of the Constitution is of historic importance and is a product of social and political fermentation of many years. In response to its election commitments and in view of the greatest political consensus possible, in order of course to bring together the required majority of the two-member parliamentarians, the Government tabled this bill, which for the first time enables Greeks, who are abroad, to exercise their right to vote in the national elections, in the European elections and in the national referendums. Although more than a hundred and fifteen countries worldwide have anticipated the participation of their citizens, who are abroad in the national elections, Greece has not established this possibility, although the ties are narrow and inseparable with the Greeks of the Diaspora. The first part of the bill stipulates that Greek citizens, who on election day are temporarily or permanently abroad and are already on the electoral lists of municipalities on the Greek territory, have the right to register on the special lists of voters abroad in order to exercise their electoral right when the following two conditions are met: First condition is the last thirty-five years from the date of submission of the application for registration in the special lists to have stayed for a total of two years in Greece, either continuous or intermittent and of course the two-year stay can be demonstrated by public documents. The second condition is that they should have made an income tax declaration in the current year or in the past. Dependent family members are excluded from the restriction if they have not completed the 30th year of age and have submitted a first-degree relative statement in the current or the previous year. An application for entry in the special electoral lists of foreign voters shall be made electronically with regard to the person concerned and the criteria required shall be determined once when the application has been entered and 10 years after the application has been accepted. The Ministry of Interior will operate a special app to registering, changing data and deleting from the special electoral lists of foreign voters. The Government's desire for a broad cross-party consensus was also expressed by the immediate acceptance of the proposal to establish a special inter-group committee to examine objections, to carry out sample checks on applications and to deliver an opinion on any necessary detail. The vote is self-determination in special elections, which are set up in embassies, buildings of Greek authorities, offices of sacred temples, shops of Greek clubs or organizations. In the polling stations, the number of voters will be between forty and six hundred. The voters vote in favour of the combination, without the right to a cross-reference. The vote of the Greeks abroad shall be counted against the overall election result. The suburbs become fifteen. At this point, the government, recognizing the right to vote for the Greeks, added to Article 32 rules so that one fifth of the voters would come from the special electoral lists abroad and at least one of them would be placed in the first three positions of the ballot. A great debate also caused the time and place of sorting and counting foreign votes. In the counterpart for their counting in Greece, the relevant Minister replied, stressing that this solution was chosen to avoid the risk of leaking any results at a time when the electoral process was not completed in Greece. Furthermore, it is important to provide, in Article 6, for Greek seafarers to vote outside the Greek territory with their naval brochure in the nearest electoral section. The second part of the bill attempts to simplify the electoral process by exploiting new technologies. Article 25 defines a specific voting day the last Sunday before the 30 days of the presidential decree is completed. Article 29 establishes an electronic procedure for the submission of applications and Article 33 simplifys the procedure for waiving posts which are an election blunt. Article 38 provides for the standard request for compensation to the members of the committee responsible. We all know that so far there have been elections with only a judicial representative. Article 43 shall facilitate the procedure for the submission of applications for a successor. I conclude with the conviction that this bill will gather the vote of almost all the parties represented in this House. This opportunity must not be lost. The first step has been taken.

Finally, I would like to say that at last the water has entered the treasury of history and it is certain that it will lead to restrictions, exceptions and exclusions in the course of time. Fortunately - and I want to be fair to this - the proposal for a law incorporates some of the main positions of the committee, the Ministry of Interior and the legislative proposal of SYRIZA, among them, the self-determination and the adoption of clear criteria. We would have saved time if the New Democracy at the outset realised that this was what respect for legal order and common sense was imposed. There have been a large number of states, both European and non-European, which have been applying the letter vote procedure for decades without having had any significant problem. This safeguard is the most important, because in this way Greeks acquire voice and therefore they can put forward positions and demands which they could not put forward if only the vote were to ensure that Members were elected in a constituency. This is alarming, since it shows a tendency to alienate even hostility, which will probably exacerbate this bill, since it is in the right direction, but reduces the number of those who could vote to a large extent under the conditions it lays down. We should note here that migration due to the crisis is the third in a row in Modern Greek History and the second largest, since it was estimated in 2018 to six hundred thousand people, but without the trend being halted, especially in young people, since conditions in our economy are not improved. And according to data from the Central Association of Chambers of Greece, we have left our country a hundred and eighty thousand graduates, while 76% of today's teenagers are also thinking about immigrating. [[At this point, the Member, Mr Vassiliou Villiardos, presents for the minutes the above document, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] Obviously, the situation is extremely worrying when it comes to the future of Greece, how much more so since deaths go far beyond births with demographics to develop into one of the biggest problems in our country. In combination with the ever-increasing change in the population from the migratory influx of Asian and Africans, Greece's future is not being erased prosperous. While we must not forget that the growth of a country's economy depends entirely on the number and quality of its inhabitants. On the other hand, the assessment of the size of the family depends on how each individual determines Greekity, given that not all Greeks have Greek passports. However, according to estimates of the General Secretariat of the Hellenic Republic, more than five million Greek citizens live in forty countries of the world, of which three million in the United States, one million in Europe, including Russia and the eastern countries, seven hundred thousand in Australia, three hundred thousand in Canada, one hundred thousand in Asia-Africa and sixty thousand in Central and South America. Of the three million, of course, in the United States, only three hundred thousand speak Greek. However, 70% of the Greeks of the second generation and 60% of the third are aware of their Greek origin and state proud of it while they have adopted Greek traditions. In conclusion, with regard to the basic arguments put forward against the vote of the Greeks, I will say the following: firstly, the payment of taxes on their part, so that their vote is more responsible, is wrong, since taxes on Greeks abroad are not rewardable, such as taxes on all of us. For example, they do not use the public infrastructure for which taxes are paid. So it's not right for anyone to charge them. Secondly, the argument that out-of-presidents may overreact elections does not seem to be intimidating other countries. Like the example of Portugal that 14% of its citizens live abroad or 9.1% of Poland, which also lives abroad when both of these countries allow their immigrants to vote. Finally, according to the results of the European elections allowed to vote abroad, the results were not so different from those of Greece. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u159	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I come to the step taken by the national Parliament with a sense of responsibility, but also of deep satisfaction, because we are now able to do to a significant extent to make it easier for Greeks to vote abroad from the country in which they are located. A request for decades of Greek immigrant organisations, a constitutionally unequivocal democratic right for citizens living abroad, takes place. The vote on this bill today is very national and political because of its content, but also because it takes place at a very special time for our country. Greece, with the political change marked by the elections of 7 July, is orbiting significant economic growth and reconstruction. After a decade of deep crisis and false divisional dilemmas that have brought back the country, optimism is coming back for today and tomorrow in the country and society. At the same time, however, we are under severe threat from neighbouring Turkey, which is systematically violating our sovereign rights and the country is receiving waves of illegal immigrants and refugees, with the European borders closed behind us, and is also seriously threatened by the demographic problem that is evolving into a time bomb. We need as much as ever to implement an integrated national strategy that Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis has developed and promoted. A national strategy that assumes consents and shocks, particularly with regard to policy areas that are directly related to our national interests. In the context of this national strategy, it is absolutely essential to mobilise global Hellenism and, at the same time, to implement an obscene and essential concern for younger generations within and outside Greece. This also requires, in turn, the continuation of the fundamental link between Greeks abroad and their homeland. And this bond is other than their self-evident, democratic and constitutionally guaranteed right to be able to vote for Greece from the country they are in. For these reasons, the vote today by all the members of the National Representation of this bill will be an important act that symbolises the national unity, but also the basic credibility of our political system towards the Greek people and against the democratic Hellenism. It took forty-five years to allow the political system of the country to do so today. Ladies and gentlemen, the interest of the great liberal party in facilitating the vote of Greeks abroad is continuous and time-consuming. In the first decade of this century, the Government of New Democracy with Prime Minister Kostas Karamanlis and Minister of Interior, the current President of the Republic of Prokopi Pavlopoulou tabled a proposal to facilitate Greeks abroad to vote from the country in which they live. It was not possible at that time to gather the required majority of the two-thirds Members, and neither did the corresponding efforts made by Antonis Samaras in the very difficult Memorandum years that ruled the country. Since taking over the leadership of the New Democracy, Kyriakos Mitsotakis has made systematic efforts to facilitate the vote of Greeks abroad. The New Democracy as a Civic Opposition has tabled legislative proposals, which have never agreed to be discussed in the years of previous governance. And so we have arrived nowadays when the crucial dilemma was set before us: to move through compromises and convergings with the other parties, seeking a common place with them, in order to gather the two-voting votes, or to insist to the end in our opinion, and therefore to relocate the opportunity to vote for a law that produces results? The decision was clear and firm. We wanted and want to achieve an agreement that takes us forward, enables the Greeks abroad to vote from the country in which they are located, an agreement which will be operational. That is why the Prime Minister took an important decision which announced on 31 July to separate the debate on the electoral system, the next parliamentary elections, the general elections, from the debate on the Greeks abroad. This decision greatly facilitated subsequent developments and the Prime Minister at the meetings with the political leaders on 11 October clearly set a single red line, the equivalent of the Greeks' vote abroad with that of all of us who are voting domestically. It is this, ladies and gentlemen of the Anti-Population Officer, to allow me to say, and it is the main difference between the Haritsis finding and the content of today's bill that this law that you too are now accepting, gives the opportunity for an equitable election to be held in the country with the right to vote on Greeks abroad. When this red line was put on the table, the possibility of agreement between the parties, which would lead to two hundred votes, was also demonstrated. Efforts were followed through the meetings of the Intergroup, held on 16, 21 and 22 October. In these discussions the New Democracy described its main position, that all Greeks vote without discrimination and exceptions and that the best method is that of the letter vote. It was clear that with this position we had no chance at all of bringing together two hundred votes, as in this respect they only converged two parties, the Change Movement and the Greek Solution, which had a very positive contribution to this case from the outset. However, in the discussions of the intergroup, we have entered openly, without prejudice, without any idea. The only red line was what the Prime Minister said. We have followed and analysed very carefully the structured arguments of the other parties, parties with which we are divided into deep ideological differences, such as the Communist Party, but we are discussing with them, with respect and, above all, with credibility, seeking to understand the causes and reasons of their own positions and arguments. We have done so in a very persistent and systematic way until we have reached an agreement, which is, in many cases, described in the bill that we are called upon to vote today. The solution we give is not ideal, it is not ideal. But it is the only politically realistic and the only one that can take us forward. It is clear that this content is the point of agreement on the current political system. They contributed positively to this, of course, by ranking all parties with their representatives in the intergroup, and I thank them for that. It is, of course, ahead of this point of the political system in the past few years and decades, but it is very far behind where the Greek people's perceptions are, and especially the Greeks, who would like to avoid any exceptions and discrimination. With the solution we are giving, however, we are achieving something that I consider to be important. All young people up to thirty years of age can vote from the country in which they are situated, in fact without any condition at all. In this way, hundreds of thousands of young children who left our country either to study or to career in order to find a better solution to the years of the crisis, hundreds of thousands of children who are the body and hope of Greece today and tomorrow have the right to vote. Our concern for this generation is not an opportunity, it is a priority for this government and it is expressed with important combined policies relating to employment, support for young couples and family in our country. At the same time, I stress that there is no limit to the law on when someone left the country, in relation to his right to vote from the country he is from. Whether someone left 20, thirty, forty or fifty years from home, the requirement demanded by the law is to be able to prove by public documents that in the last thirty-five years there have been at least two and not necessarily on a continuous basis, in which he has been in Greece, either to work in the private or public sector, to develop business, or to study, to attend a public or private technical vocational school, or to carry out his military obligation towards the home country. In reality, therefore, with the existence of these conditions, the vast majority of Greeks abroad have the opportunity to participate in the relevant regulations. The conditions described for an active tax declaration must be clear that in no case have they come, because we are interested in the class mark in the course, life and identity of every Greek citizen, but simply because political forces have placed the need to demonstrate the awareness of the citizens' interests in terms of the political situation of the country, as well as a fundamental economic institution with the country. This regulation, therefore, concerns hundreds of thousands of Greeks abroad. It matters how many of them will want to take advantage of this possibility. This depends on the messages that the National Representation and the political system will receive today and the next time, on the information we have to make to Greeks abroad, and of course on the attitude that will be taken by the Greek migrants' organizations and forces. For this additional reason today, we are all called upon to vote with force positively and decisively on this bill, each exceeding its own separate proposals. Let us vote unanimously by sending a very strong message to our compatriots all over the earth. Greece embraces them, loves them, as they love their homeland, and today we take a step together to satisfy their basic democratic right. Ladies and gentlemen, the JRC's proposal has three main priorities in order to exercise the electoral right at the place of residence. The first axis is the existence of real economic and social links with Greece, such as tax obligations and not just a general mental bond. After all, even at the core of the urban democratic perception of formal equality of voting rights is the correlation of the right to vote in the parliamentary elections on the one hand and the existence of obligations on the other, that is to say, having the consequences of any political choice. The second axis is the existence of a maximum time absence from the country or a minimum presence time in the country. The third axis of our proposal is the personal presence in election parties abroad, which guarantees the secrecy and the validity of the vote. But above all it guarantees effective political participation in an electoral process, contrary to all these well-known theories of e-democracy, e-electoral or digital democracy, which are very fashionable lately. These proposals do not constitute a restriction or a loss of electoral rights. Those who do not have the conditions to join these lists do not mean they lose the right to vote. They are simply conditions for the exercise of the electoral right through their inclusion in the special electoral lists abroad. In particular, the European Council must clarify in relation to Turkey's illegal memorandum with Libya that the European Union and the Member States do not recognise the Turkish - Libyan memorandum, do not recognise maritime zones that have been illegally demarcated. And secondly, that it will impose sanctions against acting in a illegally demarcated zone, which concerns a Member State of the European Union. This will be an important step in creating a wider international front for our rights that we need today. In this context, both you, Prime Minister, the People's Party and I in the Socialist Party will take the necessary steps to achieve the best possible outcome. Secondly, strengthening all the cooperation schemes built up in the Eastern Mediterranean, but also bilateral initiatives with countries that concern stability and security in the region from Russia to China and every country that has interests in our region. Thirdly, the immediate qualitative upgrading of the national deterrent forces. We have the advantage of having a high-level experienced and reliable staff in the armed forces. We must therefore provide them with all the means to make it clear that anyone who applies a finger to our national sovereign rights will lose their hand. [[Applause from the Wing of Change Movement]] Fourthly, we must seek to demarcate maritime zones with the border countries. Recognise the jurisdiction of the International Court of Justice in The Hague, start talks on the drafting of the promise to lead us to settle the dispute. All this gains strength and produces results on the basis of unity. The Greeks want peace, we want good neighbourlyness, understanding and cooperation. Our national red line is defending our sovereign rights. Anyone who wants to exercise violence in order to trespass our legitimate sovereign rights must know from now on that he will pay dearly the price. Ladies and gentlemen, today's sitting, I dare say, is historic. The vote of the Greek foreigners is finally taking place. It has taken eighteen years since the decisive initiative of the PASOK during the 2001 constitutional review that gave the green light to move forward. It is self-evident that Greeks may vote. As it goes without saying that they have the right to vote, the opportunity to vote on children, the young scientists who have stayed out in the crisis in recent years seeking a better future. It is their right, it is a matter of national importance, it is our duty to ensure it. We took a very clear position from the first moment. We suggested that all Greek residents should have the right to vote if they are registered on electoral lists and polls. In order to participate in the elections from their place of residence or work, we asked for an update of their registration with a statement to the local consulates and embassies of the Hellenic Republic. We call upon them to be able to express their participation by written vote and procedures that will ensure its validity fully. We have made it clear that it is necessary to count on the overall national election result. We have made it quite clear from the outset our proposal for the real representation of the results we have asked for through the creation of a new single region of Greek foreign residents who will elect MPs through the special votes of the parties. With these proposals we sought the widest possible participation of Greek foreigners in the parliamentary elections, full transparency in voting procedures, representation as a distinctive category in the House through MPs who will be elected and represented. The way to dialogue, however, was not easy at all. From the outset, New Democracy did not consult its proposals and was limited to leakages, apparently choosing its logic by seeing and doing, and SYRIZA made the unprecedented political and institutional person suggesting that the Greeks vote outside the national outcome of the elections should not be taken into account, thus completely violating the constitutional principle of equality of vote and confronting the migrant Greeks as second-class citizens. Alongside the beginning and until the end, SYRIZA objected to the dialectic vote and to the facilitation of fraud in the exercise of their electoral right. The JRC also laid down a number of conditions and conditions which significantly limited the body of the foreign electorate. With all the above it is easy to conclude that exactly when we underlined the self-evidentness and fought for it, others put an asterisk and questions. When we supported the universality of voting others laid down conditions that led to exclusion. And when we insisted toward the end in the letter vote others clearly refused it and got to the point of vetoing. When we put on the table not only the vote of the deputies, but also their representation with the election of Members representing them, others suggested that they should not even be included in the national outcome. Because party legs prevailed. It is, of course, a start. Ladies and gentlemen, the provisions which will be adopted today by a large majority incorporate four key points. I don't suppose you'll change your mind. If at least I judge what you have said in committee, do not surprise us. Convergences have been noted in four points, which form the backbone of the reform. The first and most importantly, one unnegotiable condition set by the government by a majority: the absolute measurement of the vote of the non-national Greek voters in the national total result of the elections. [[Applause from the New Democracy wing]] I wonder how any other alternative proposal could be considered, which is fundamentally in line with the requirements of the Constitution. You know that any different approach would be directly unconstitutional, would be contrary to the rule of democratic representation and the equivalence of votes. Secondly, our compatriots will be able to exercise their right to vote in elections with at least forty voters. These will be created in embassies, consulates, as well as in facilities of impoverished persons, as well as in other buildings in the countries where the distance from the home of the national authorities is high. Thirdly, they increase from twelve to fifteen Member States, including non-border voters. There will be candidates and candidates for the dispersion. Any other solution concerning the crusade of foreign voters would create insurmountable technical problems. It's your suggestion, Mrs. Gennamas. I don't have a hard time recognizing it. That's right. It ensures that parties elected by at least three Member States will elect a representative of the Greeks from abroad as a minimum. Fourthly, it is stated that in order for Greeks abroad to be facilitated to vote, two conditions are set. I want to make it clear from the outset that these conditions, which have created some reactions from Greeks who have been missing for many years, but are still on the electoral lists, have not been made - I will say again - by the government majority. I am referring to the obligation to stay at least two years in Greece over the last thirty-five years and to be tax-based in the last or current year, unless they are less than thirty years old. They were preconditions set by other parties. It was a prerequisite for us to reach the consensus that we needed to go beyond the limit of two hundred Members. They were shot by the Communist Party and SYRIZA. That is why these restrictions exist today. Three parties, the Greek Solution, the Change Movement and the New Democracy did not consider them necessary. I want to be quite clear to those who are listening to us that these restrictions were the result of the necessary retreat that the government majority had to make in order to reach the number of two hundred Members of Parliament, so that this important reform could take place. The certification process will be simple. The foreign voter will be electronically entered in the respective electoral list, either by submitting the necessary documents or by allowing the public services to draw them from their own databases. With a simple document, that is to say, a certificate of study, a payment of contributions, a military severance office, the Greeks who live permanently outside the border will now be able to vote from their place of residence. Of course, everyone, I will say again, always retains the right to vote in the ballot in Greece. We're not talking about an additional right. We are talking about a facilitation in the exercise of the electoral right. I'll say it again. This quadrant was not the best solution. It was, however, the only realistic and directly applicable solution. Otherwise, the major would be subjected to the minor and instead of making one more important step, in my view, in the face of the decay, we would retreat two steps into the name of a leap we would never have done. In other words, we would not be able to vote on Greeks living permanently abroad, as was the case for almost half a century, and now we have a law, even with some discounts. All the generation of Brain Drain, all the young children who left Greece in the years of the crisis, automatically fall under the provisions of the law and many of the Greeks who left the country earlier and who have been resident for many years abroad will be able to make use of the right to vote from their permanent residence. Ladies and gentlemen, this bill can be signed as a democratic victory, since it substantially broadens the right to vote. Because you know very well that many foreign Greeks were unable to return to Greece, even if they wished. This exclusion from the opportunity to participate in the electoral process has also had a strong class mark. We must not forget that. Returning to Greece a weekend to exercise its electoral right is a fairly precise process and the main obstacle to many of our fellow citizens abroad was precisely the cost. So this goes beyond today. That's why it's a democratic victory. It substantially broadens the right to vote. It is a parliamentary success, a parliamentary victory, since it shows the common place of different perceptions. First of all, however, it is a national victory. It performs a debt that Greece had vis-à-vis its children. It reconnects the Greeks of the world to their national homes, gives them a reason for what is happening at home. It thus rekindles the interest in the homeland to older and younger generations, for our parents, our uncles who have stumbled there, but also for children, the nieces that either study abroad or left the years of the crisis. The importance of the vote of the Greeks abroad extends even further the national look, deepens national consciousness. Because it once again unites the Greeks of the world of many millions, thus increasing their power. And their old extrovert, which has always raised the country and washed its influence on the ends of the world. To use the words of the intellectual Stelios Ramfou: "We pass from the limited Greekism to the true Hellenism of the open horizons." Above all, at this delicate juncture, today's sitting of Parliament is a message of consensus, unity and determination, a message that we are few to be divided, which is why we choose not to be scattered. So global Hellenism is here and I invite you to prove it with your vote in a moment. Thank you.

The Special Committee has come about in the same context as all the respective institutional bodies in the case of countries with a large number of impunities, such as Greece, in the so-called 'closed representation system', with a predetermined, closed number of seats corresponding to voters abroad. It is the model that adopts countries with significant dispersions, such as France, Italy and Portugal. It is the model that prejudges best practice, the National Committee on Human Rights and the Venice Commission, the Council of Europe. It is the model that meets the positions of the CSCE, the Council of Deputies of Hellenism. We have therefore come to the public debate from the standpoint of counter-population, bringing the proposal to which this committee concludes.
