I want to be clear. This proposal is not sufficient in our proposal. Not because it is our own proposal and I must support it, but because, on the one hand, it is our proposal that provided for an independent right to vote other than the electorate and, on the other hand, because our proposal was, as I have already said, the proposal adopted by all the national and international institutions. Nevertheless, we have come to the debate, assuming that a step has to be taken, we have to close an issue that remains unregulated for forty years and that we have to stand as political parties, as a political leadership worthy of the expectations of the deceit but also of the value that these people have as ambassadors to our country, our language, our culture, our potential in the global sphere. We have therefore identified, Minister, four main problems in the bill that you have brought, and we have proposed four corresponding solutions. In your honor, you accepted both, even partially. But there are still two other issues, top in our view, which should, even at the last minute, be understood by your Government. The first concerns the updating of the special electoral lists abroad. Preferring on the draft one-off listing and maintaining the same electoral lists for an eight-year period - you corrected it for decades - it does not guarantee for the future the existence of a genuine link between the voter and the country, that is, the real bond consisting of two-year stay in Greece within thirty-five years and the submission of a tax declaration in the same or the previous year. And I think our proposal solves the problem. Our proposal is that the special electoral lists should be updated, either annually or on a two-year basis. The second key issue is that the bill includes wide authorisations to the Minister of the Interior to regulate key issues, such as the process of listing and the way the elector is certified. But if these matters can be regulated by the Minister with ministerial decisions, then the requirement of the Constitution for approval by the two-thirds of the House is the same, which is why we propose, as a solution, that all the relevant ministerial decisions be adopted in agreement, not simple, as provided for in the bill, and only for some decisions by them, not all, with an agreement and with a majority of two-thirds of the Special Permanent Committee on Hellenism of the Diversity or the Intergroup. I would therefore ask you to take some steps to resolve these issues so that we can vote in favour of the bill, not only at first, but also in all articles. I think that would be a positive development. ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u206	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, one of the central political objectives of the New Democracy Government is the return of the Greeks who left Greece during the crisis. Our most ambitious aim is to repatriate and reconnect the Greeks of the Diaper, to convince all migrants that the mother is open and ready to welcome them back, that we want them back, that Greece is turning into a modern European state, a rule of law that can satisfy their demands, a state that can treat the citizens with respect, such as most modern states. These two central political objectives have led us to the need to seek wider political consensus on the issue of facilitating the electoral right of non-Greek voters. The first reason was purely practical, since the Constitution requires an increased majority of two-thirds of the members of parliament to apply the law. The second main reason was purely national. The opportunity shouldn't have been lost. Because we consider it incomprehensible in the year 2020 that we cannot as a state, as a political system, agree in a way that will enable Greeks abroad to exercise their right to vote. And we're talking about people on the electoral lists. If they take the plane and come to Greece, they can vote. And why do we think it's incomprehensible? Because the message we send to these people is that we've forgotten them, that we don't care if they keep their ties with their homeland, that we don't care if they come back. So the Greek state is not meant to send such a message. We are obliged to do everything politically possible to resolve a matter that has been of concern to us for decades. Of course, the agreement of the four plus one points reached by the Minister for the Interior meets our main objective, but not entirely. The first reason is because there have been several restrictions. The conditions for exercising their right to vote from the place of residence we consider that they could have been avoided. But we are sure that the Greeks from abroad will receive the message, they will overcome the limitations, they will come to the polls and, in their vote, they will prove that they remain close to us in every way. Thus, they will legalize the next House to amend the bill and lift some of the conditions for their registration in the special catalogues abroad. The second and most important reason why we are not entirely satisfied with the agreement is that the right is restricted to voting for the State vote, even though this is extended from twelve to fifteen seats and not for the vote on their constituency. This could be done in practical terms by means of the letter of vote, which was the government's original proposal. Otherwise, we should set up, as we said, fifty-nine ballots in every election centre. The second is impossible. The first one didn't want the antipopulation parties. So we've come up with this compromise. Ladies and gentlemen, I think we are taking a step forward. And I feel great joy as a Member of the national Parliament more than a majority MEP just when we were all taking this step together. I cannot imagine - I imagine all the colleagues who are voting for the bill - that we would not do so, that we would not manage to stand up to the circumstances, that we would not be able to send the Greeks all over the world the message that Greece is changing. Because I imagine that we all want Greece to leave misery, introverty and crisis behind and to become a normal modern state, a state similar to what Greeks abroad chose as a temporary place of residence, a state that wants them to return and co-formulate the new beginning for us and our children. Thank you.

Today we are called upon to discuss and approve the government's proposal and the way in which the Greeks who live outside Greece are involved in the electoral procedures of the country. The request for Greeks to vote abroad is a request for many years, but it was never satisfied either by New Democracy or by PASOK. The first essential effort to facilitate Greek foreigners to participate in the Greek elections was made by the SYRIZA government, without micro-party practices, with the establishment of a scientific committee, which resulted in a very specific proposal. SYRIZA's proposal to create a closed system of participation by all Greeks abroad, which would lead to the representation of the family without party controversy and without dividing Hellenism, was not accepted for reasons that the Government of New Democracy has not explained to us and has not presented to us. The original proposal by the New Democracy - which, fortunately, is not raised in the debate - poses significant risks to the unity of Hellenism in general. It could cause serious irreversibility to exercise the responsibility for governance, without practically agreeing to the Greek people's agreement, which is directly influenced by government policy being pursued. The New Democracy, even lately, perceived this risk and practically adopted the proposal of counter-population and, in particular, the JRC, which basically has its starting point in depreciating this risk, which would inevitably create social conflicts. The proposal that eventually comes for discussion following the reactions of Officer Response is clearly more secure. It's an improvement, of course. It ensures, however, to a large extent that the participants will even have relative contact with the reality in Greece and ensure that the representatives of the Greeks abroad are also represented in the House. Despite the distortions created by the government's final proposal, it is clearly better than its original, sloppy and obviously dangerous initial position. In this sense, an important step is being taken towards the participation of Greeks abroad in our national elections. This even retarded welfare of participation, of course, is not enough in itself to strengthen the ties of the democratic Hellenism with Greece. Other provisions are needed, such as the support of Greek schools, the strengthening of Greek language and history courses, the implementation of actions and the taking of initiatives to prove to the respondent Greeks that there is a country that cares about them. In any case, today there is one step to keep Greeks in touch with Greece and at least the younger generations of immigrants who have been forced or chose to leave the country because of the economic crisis and the social consequences that it has caused. They are mainly Greek citizens who have already had the right to vote in Greece, who are aware of the real situation of Greek society and many of them are keen to return to a country that will not deprive them of dignity. They will undoubtedly not reduce the abstention rate in the latest electoral procedures, since it is difficult and particularly expensive for a Greek abroad to come to Greece just to vote. However, the request from the Greek community is partially met. By voting for this bill some and only some people are satisfied and not the unscrupulous Greeks who could have the right to vote. The proposal that SYRIZA had drawn up with the distinct representation of Greeks abroad in the Greek Parliament would have offered the right to vote to all the Greeks who would wish it without artificial separations. It would allow these citizens' voices to be translated into Greece and, above all, it would not have caused the separation of the Greek-democratic on the basis of party disputes. This democratic proposal, unfortunately, cannot be accepted by the ruling party, which reminds us at every opportunity that it considers it incomprehensible that there should be a parliament which will not be fully identified by legislative and executive powers. In any case, it is a positive development, and even under certain excessive conditions, that these Greeks of the diaspora can participate as many as possible in national elections. It is also good that this issue does not ultimately become a field of harsh confrontation since there is a common will of all the political forces and that those moves which make it easier for Greeks abroad to participate on this basis should be made. In conclusion, I would like once again to point out that Turkey's risk is real and we need more than ever an active foreign policy. We do not need the statements of support, the verbal convictions and the final provision of space and time to question our sovereign rights in practice. Greece cannot stand appallingly against the aggression of Turkey nor can illegal activities be tolerated either on the Greek continental continent, the Greek islands and the Greek EEZ, or on the Cypriot EEZ, on plot 7 or other plots as described by the Republic of Cyprus. The government must take substantial initiatives. Yesterday it was shown that the political system is aware of the risks and the whole of the opposition parties are moving despite the individual disagreements or criticisms in the same direction. The Greek people must be informed and we must make proper use of any diplomatic means that can save an accident and at the same time defend our sovereign rights in full. The fact that the New Democracy initially underestimated Turkey's expansionist policy is a point of criticism. However, this is not the time to serve as a political controversy. The data have now been changed, and we need to make full use of the results and potential created by Greece's multi-dimensional foreign policy on Alexis Tsipras. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u232	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, following the vote in favour of the revision of Article 54 of the Constitution, the way in which the law has been passed facilitated for Greek foreign residents to vote in elections and referendums, without coming to Greece. This is a self-evident right for Greeks who live abroad, constitutionally guaranteed and who unfortunately could not be implemented by the previous governments. This request for decades is being made today by the current government in keeping with its election commitments - in addition, as it had made this issue a first priority - to implement it by means of this bill with the desire to vote, as the Prime Minister has said many times, by three hundred Members. And it has emerged throughout the procedures that the current government, in its dealings with the issue without regularization and expediency, has shown its desire for broad acceptance, exhausting all margins of consensus. Indeed, this bill is a product of extensive consultation, it adopts all the essential points of the parties and contains all the guarantees to be voted on by Parliament as a whole. As has been said, the vote of foreign voters is not a matter for today. And I am not simply referring to the fact that the constitutional legislature has defined it as a possibility already since 1975, but also to the fact that other countries have such potential since the beginning of the 20th century. At the moment, the vote of the foreign voters is guaranteed in at least one hundred and fifteen countries. The forms offered are the vote in consular offices, the letter of vote, the vote by delegation and, in some cases, the electronic vote. As far as our country is concerned, the 2001 Review changed two things in the contested provision. Firstly, analogous to the majority needed to change the electoral system in the next election, the provision that the law in question needs a majority of 2/3 of the total number of Members, thus a minimum of two hundred votes, was added. Secondly, at the end of Article 51(4), the statement was added that the principle of the simultaneous holding of the elections does not prevent the exercise of their voting rights by written vote or other appropriate means. It goes without saying that the general principles laid down in Article 51, namely the directity - that is to say, not by a representative - of universality - counts, that is, the vote and measures - and the secrecy of the vote, and the compulsion of the exercise of the electoral right - without any sanctions - are applicable to the voters. On the basis of these constitutional principles, this draft law regulating the constitutional provision for the exercise of elections to the European Parliament and the holding of a national referendum provides for the following: It should be noted that in addition to consulates, other areas including the public areas of the country where the voters live will be used. In order to create an election division, forty voters are required and the highest number is six hundred. This number facilitates the creation of many electoral centres and ensures the secrecy of the vote. The vote of the voters exercising the right to vote in the above elections will be equal to the overall election result and thus to the total distribution of seats. Also, the voters in the foreign sections will choose without a cross-reference to the party's voters who want, at the same time, to increase the number of MPs from twelve to fifteen. Moreover, the Government, in fact, demonstrated the desire for the widest acceptance of this draft law, proceeded to the adoption of up to three of the fifteen members of the prestige vote to come from the migrants and one of them placed in the first three positions. Regarding the right to register on foreign electoral lists, voters have the right to meet cumulatively the following criteria: In the last thirty-five years they have lived for at least two years in Greece and have submitted a statement either in the current or the previous tax year. It should be noted that a tax declaration is not required if the voter has not completed thirty years of life and has made a tax declaration of relative I in the current and previous tax year. To conclude, I would like to stress that the fulfilment of the constitutional requirement is an institutional and technically overtime demand and obligation of the state against an important category of Greek citizens. This legislative initiative is now of major political importance, as it is not only about homosexuals who have left Greece for decades but also about five hundred thousands of citizens who have been forced to leave the country in the years of the crisis. Let us not forget - and I say this as a reassurance of any concern expressed by the representatives of the revenues - that this bill is a huge legislative step from scratch and that it can well be improved in the future. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u237	Mr. Kamines has the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u238	Ladies and gentlemen, it has had to be about half a century. Two revisions of the Constitution were needed to enable us to vote today on the law whereby non-stated Greeks would be able to participate in the elections. The reason for this delay, I believe, is obvious. There are so many Greeks abroad, that for decades and regardless of a parliamentary majority the fear that their turnout to the polls would be too wide to experience the election result inside the country. For this reason, in 2001, the Constitutional Review set the highest limit of 2/3 of the total number of Members in order to vote on the relevant law. It should be noted that such a high threshold is uncompromising, and on the contrary even in procedures of top state of importance, such as the Revision of the Constitution and the election of the President of the Republic, the Constitution is now limited to a smaller or much smaller majority. As we all know here, in order to reach the agreement of the parliamentary groups needed a second constitutional review today, it was only a few weeks ago. We reviewed Article 54 in order to establish there the possibility for the legislator to impose certain reasonable restrictions on the exercise of the electoral right. We know, however, that we have revised the wrong article, not of course, but of indifference, because we had not taken care of the House to include Article 51 in the articles to be revised. We have thus reached the paradox of reviewing an indirect article of the Constitution in breach of Article 110. Hours have been spent both in the previous Parliament and in the current question of the second parliament's commitment from the directions of the first. The subject also dealt with constitutional theory. All this controversy was eventually thrown into the gallery of the useless, since not only did we not respect the directions of the House proposed, but we overshooted the dilemma itself by reviewing, without confessing, the provisions of the Constitution, which were not even among the revised ones. The dissatisfaction of the Greek associations abroad is understandable, since the restrictions contained in the bill exclude most. They're right. We, in the Change Movement, have, from the outset, supported the unrestricted participation of all on the basis of the democratic principle, the principle of universality. Could reality be different? The answer is negative. Why? For there are the parliamentary powers, which are essentially vetoing because of the increased majority. As mentioned in the detailed report by the Venice Commission, however, there are restrictions in many countries with a large number of frauds. In fact, in some of them, such as Cyprus, Malta and Ireland, there is neither the constitutional nor legislative provision for the vote on redundancies. In us, the number of evacuations is not large just because of successive migrations. It is further and undetermined because of the law of blood, which traditionally prevails in Greek Law of Citizenship. Anyone who proves Greek origin, even if it is overcrowded by many generations, is entitled to the Greek nationality. However, as the relatively recent change in German citizenship law suggests, the absolute supremacy of blood law everywhere retreats. It does not respond to modern global conditions of human mobility. Especially for Greece, which in recent decades has been converted into a host country, the law of citizenship has been established in obsolete data from other times. In a way, ladies and gentlemen, we are on account of blood law in the face of a de facto enforcement of the choices of the joint legislator, through the Code of Nationality, to the detriment of the revised legislator. In a quasi-conversion, not of the formal hierarchy of the rules of law, but of their effective effect. The written vote has been taken. We must realise that we are basically complementing the second decade of the 21st century. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to agree with most of the people who have been ahead of us. ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u240	Ladies and gentlemen, to give the Greek Parliament the right for Greek citizens to vote in our national elections from the place of their permanent residence is a question of the quality of our democracy. This bill marks a leading institutional division and is also an indicator of the maturity of our political system. It is not a compromise product, nor a transaction, as some have argued, but above all it is a matter of principle. We are therefore called upon to vote on a national issue tonight, a self-evident fundamental right, in which it is inappropriate to crush into the millstones of micro-participatoryism, let alone be misled by those who wrongly believe that Greeks abroad will become a party army. For us in the New Democracy, the exercise of the right to vote of Greeks who are abroad on the day of the parliamentary elections has been a direct priority since the start. We raised the issue emphatically from the pre-election period and from the next election, as a government, we have taken all the necessary initiatives to make use of the dynamism and the clean eyes of the Greeks. We want to show our compatriots abroad that Greece, their homeland, did not forget them, as well as that Greece stops hurting them all the time. First of all, the possibility of voting from their residence will enable thousands of Greeks abroad to reconnect with their homeland. I am referring not only to the thousands who have emigrated to the years of the crisis, but also to those who have been missing for many years are at risk of being cut off from their roots. In this way they will feel even more strongly that they should be involved in the country's recovery effort, that they become the best ambassadors of Hellenism. Ladies and gentlemen, the vote of the frustrations is increasing Greece and is not a shape of speech. It ensures equality of opportunity and turns the House into an expressor, not only of the will of our people, but of universal Hellenism. We do not mean to support in our public positions that the Greeks of the diaspora are the most powerful element of our nation and that there are political parties that refuse them to participate in the national elections from their home country. It does not mean appealing to them to any difficulties and refusing them to make an active contribution, by their vote, to improving their homeland at an economic, social and political level. I'm going to set another parameter. I hear very often colleagues from the opposition parties saying that from Greeks abroad only the about four hundred thousand are registered on the electoral lists and have Greek tax numbers. They overlook, however, that if Mother Greece shows by deeds to these people that it considers them to be active citizens, then there is no doubt that this number will increase very significantly. It also defines our Constitution in Article 108(1): The state takes care of the life of the democratic Hellenism and of maintaining its ties with the mother country. So here's our chance. In any case, the right to vote on the Greeks abroad is the most important intervention to be taken in our own country, along with all the rest, of course, the reduction of bureaucracy, the reduction of taxation, the attraction of investment, in order to achieve what we all want, growth. There must be no micropolicy on this issue. We are faced with a major challenge and all of us are called upon to write a political story. The three hundred positive votes, which would be ideal and desirable, do not only prove consent, but also seal a historic agreement, which makes it self-evident that Greeks abroad must be involved in the formation of the election result and therefore in the distribution of the three hundred seats of our Parliament. I will close, saying that a problem of decades is finally solved. Our Greek compatriots who live abroad are not second-class citizens. We do not have the right to deprive them of participation in developments in Greece. We do not have the right to forbid them by our vote to love and care for their country. Having a Greek two-place of residence does not automatically make it less Greek. And because most of us in this Chamber and millions of our compatriots watching us from television have children who have emigrated abroad for better luck, let us consider the burden and responsibility of voting tonight. By hugging the Greeks of the interior, we show them that we are counting on their participation in domestic developments and demanding at the same time, through their vote, that they express an opinion on the formation of Greece of the future, of Greece that suits us, of Greece, who knows what he wants and where he goes. Building a society of equal opportunities, we will enable every Greek and every Greek woman to unfold their talent. At the same time, we make it clear to the remote Greeks that it is really worth their hearts to be beaten for Greece and their gaze is always in the homeland, as many miles away. At the beginning of my speech, ladies and gentlemen, I stressed that facilitating the vote of the Greeks on the election day is a right. I correct it: it is an obligation of a modern democratic state. So let us put aside the parties' dividing lines and do our national duty today. The vote of the Greeks, wherever they live, cannot be divided into accusations. Parliamentary unanimity on such a critical national issue does not strengthen the government, on the contrary it symbolises the unity of the Greeks and, at the same time, highlights the dynamic of the country in a period of particularly increased challenges and demands where our sovereign rights are questioned. Thank you very much.

The intention of the Government and of the New Democracy over time was and is to facilitate Greeks living abroad so that they can exercise their electoral right unforeseen and unpredictablely from their residence, as is provided for in our Constitution. The proposal for a law, therefore, to facilitate the exercise of the right of the relatives of the New Democracy to vote for the first time enables everyone on the electoral lists to be able to exercise their electoral right on local diplomatic and consular authorities. The New Democracy, with this proposal for a law, wants to facilitate all our fellow citizens who live abroad or serve to a Greek authority abroad as well as to Greek seafarers. Let us not forget the numerous community of Greek citizens living outside the country's borders today, which has been formed by both Greek and migrant waves during the economic crisis, which has to be a community that has been subject to rules in the form of the country's political situation. From the outset Hellenism offers valuable services to the nation and we in turn have to show how we can respond and give essential content to the constitutional principles of popular sovereignty and universality of vote. The proposed draft law regulates the constitutional provision for the exercise of the electoral right of voters who on the day of the national elections, the European elections and the holding of a national referendum. Outside the territory of a electorate is any Greek citizen who has the right to vote and is registered on the electoral lists and on the day of the election is permanently or temporarily outside Greece. Following the broad constitutional review of 2001, and in particular paragraph 4 of Article 51, the constitutional legislature introduced the need for a large majority in order for Parliament to be able to regulate the issues of non-national voters. In this Constitutional Review a interpretative statement was added to Article 54 on the basis of which restrictions may be placed on the exercise of the right to vote by foreign Greeks. The main restriction in Greek law on the right to vote concerns the exercise rather than the acquisition of the right in question by non-Greek citizens who are present permanently or temporarily Greek citizens. Specifically, under the current electoral legislation, as codified in the Presidential Decree as amended and in force, but also in the more specific provisions applicable to other national procedures in force, Greek voters exercise their right to vote in person in the elections which are formed in each municipality of the country. Because I do not want to tire of repetitions that many colleagues have mentioned all day in the restrictions imposed by the draft law in question, I would also like to give other colleagues the opportunity to speak. I would therefore like to close my speech by saying that for the first time after many decades Greece, unfortunately, because of the economic crisis, has again become an export of labour. Greek citizens left the country seeking out work and with hope one day when conditions allow them to repatriate. In these Greeks who have fresh ties to the mother country as well as to all the Greeks of the Diaspora who maintain lime the flame of Hellenism no one has the right or moral stature to raise obstacles to the bridge with Greece. International experience shows that any practical obstacles can be easily overcome if there is a political will. There is no excuse for raising the democratic deficit with time and Greek citizens, who have direct and objective ties with their homeland, being deprived of their basic rights. This is a unique opportunity to strengthen our country's democracy. I firmly believe that all parties in this House will support this proposal of the Government, because we in the New Democracy believe it is a realistic, politically viable and administratively practicable proposal. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-12-11-S1-commons.u300	Thank you, Mr President. Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, as stated in the explanatory statement, the right to vote of non-national voters is to be reserved for the purpose of forging substantial ties between Greeks and Greeks. This need becomes even more urgent because of the migration of a large number of our compatriots during the time of the crisis. It is also obvious that Greeks who permanently live abroad for objective or subjective reasons do not have the same ties with the country. However, the bill under discussion is undoubtedly a powerful lever for strengthening the ties between the Greeks and the Greek state, i.e. the organization and functioning of the state. At the same time, it is an incentive for Greeks abroad to participate and co-formulate the political life of the place. That is why this universal electoral law is fundamentally concluded with the construction of the Greeks of the diaspora, a force that I think we all feel has not been exploited to the full, despite the state's long-term efforts and certainly has much to offer. Therefore, this legislation can be the starting point for a more targeted and effective activation of the inexhaustible forces of Greeks living abroad through the design and implementation of policies that will give them access to metropolitan-center issues across the range of economic, social and political life. We must confess, ladies and gentlemen, that Greeks who live abroad feel devastated, feel that their activation becomes short-term and fragmented. Even during the debate on the draft law we have received this message on the basis of the limitations placed on the exercise of their right to vote. I do not underestimate the important initiatives that have been taken in the past and the actions that have been carried out at times. But there is no doubt a lot of room to bring our compatriots living abroad closer to us. From the work of the Special Permanent Committee on the Hellenism of the Dispersion, in which I have the honour of participating, I have recorded the dimensions of the subject and also important weaknesses, structural and operational. So, having expressed my pleasure in fulfilling this national debt, I would like to highlight a fundamental shortage that I have identified. It is noted that the relevant Ministry and the Secretariat-General do not have a consolidated and up-to-date database of Greeks residing abroad, and where such data are not suitable for organised and systematic processing. Consequently - and since the next period following the vote on the bill will start a great deal of effort to get this the greatest possible response - we have the golden opportunity to make use of the invitation to all the compatriots abroad, asking them, apart from the electoral lists, to register in a second register, the Migratory Greeks register. They may even choose areas of interest and offer to pursue a number of national objectives. In this way we will be able to draw up the first universal register of Greeks and make use of the endless forces of Hellenism in various already recognised and applied actions, such as the promotion of Philhellenicism, the support of our homogenous clubs and Church, economic diplomacy, the dissemination of culture and the Greek language, and others that will be adopted in the future. At a practical level, I recommend that any action to promote registration should be accompanied by a corresponding entry in the Universal Register of Degrees of Greeks. It is obvious that this register should have the potential and the electoral list, i.e. be open to entries, corrections and write-offs. Ladies and gentlemen, the vote on the draft law on the facilitation of non-state voters is another commitment by the government, Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis, to Greek women and Greeks, which is achieved by the exhaustion of every consensus, mainly by the government majority. [[At this point, the Honourable Member's speech bell is ringing.]] I hope, in fact, that it will soon widen its scope to include all Greeks who live abroad and be accompanied by other corresponding initiatives that grow up and strengthen our country. Thank you very much.

The self-representation of the electorate in electoral departments to be set up in embassies, consulates, landmarks or other suitable premises. It is therefore not limited to the election section in the embassy or consulate of the country, since this would deprive thousands of voters who live in cities that do not have diplomatic authority. Also, the minimum number to set up an election division is forty voters, which means that even in small towns inhabiting Greeks of the diaspora, they will be able to exercise their electoral right. Point two. The vote of the voters exercising their right to vote in the above elections will be equal to the overall election result and thus to the total distribution of seats. So they vote like every Greek. Point 3. The state MPs are growing from twelve to fifteen, and the voters will choose the party's voters who want to vote without putting a cross of preference. Our love for our country unites us, and I think that today we are all going to be winners by voting for this bill.

Thank you.
